When we think about who's really an interesting competitor, it's the pure crypto firm, the pure games firm, the pure AI firm, more than the generalist firm that's trying to cover all of that with the old structure. I think that's going to be harder for them. There's a real interesting alignment problem with going public if you're a venture capital firm, and it's as follows. If you look at Apollo, Blackstone, or any of these guys' private equity companies that have gone public, the public markets value them on their fee stream much more than on their investment returns. I think that's a safer alignment between the investors and the firms in private equity than it is in venture capital. I think in venture capital, that can get super dangerous because even at 100X, what it used to be, the entire venture capital market is not that big. The amount of capital versus the amount of great ideas, we already have more capital than great ideas. As we saw, I think, with both SoftBank and Tiger Global, if you try to change that demand supply imbalance, you just end up creating a mess. If you were public, you'd have a strong incentive to create a mess. They went big and created a mess, but you guys went as big in some ways, right? Your volume was very high, your funds raised was very high, you went big in a much better way. We didn't go $100 billion big, and then I think Tiger was raising $12 billion a year, so they were bigger than us, just technically. We scaled to basically size our funds to the market opportunities. The way we look at it is, in a two- to three-year time frame, how many great deals will we see in a category and then try to size the fund to basically cover that time period is kind of roughly how we do it. That's certainly increased both fund sizes and the number of funds over the years, but it's still really contained compared to what you'd do if you were just scaling assets. I think it's still way smaller than what Apollo or Vista or somebody would do in that kind of business. I think that misalignment is pretty tricky for venture capital to overcome. A firm that's diligent on fund size, a benchmark or stays at $500 or $250 respectively, they believe that they can get better multiples on that much smaller fund size. That's just kind of a function of, look, if there were 15 companies and now there's 150, if you had a $400 million fund, then maybe you need a $4 billion fund to do the same deals if you win the same percentage of them. I think, also, we could all get much higher salaries if we didn't organize the firm the way we did. Our mission isn't to maximize the number of money per partner. Our mission is to be the resource for building great technology companies. It's just a different point of view. For example, there's this kind of thing in venture capital that a lot of venture capitalists will say, well, spend all your time with your winners. We don't believe in that at all. The way we look at it is, number one, we're not so confident that we know who the winners are for a long time. From a competitive standpoint, our whole idea is that we sell on reputation. That's fundamentally important to our competitive advantage is to have the best reputation. We're about 5 million housing units short of what we actually need in the country. Our goal is to enable fully autonomous buildings.
